The vaults read `digest populated=10140 materials 56008794b8acf8629492df583b1e45bc occupancy b8c45d8b3dad01ef90bda339774771af` and `deep populated=856 materials 2df887b48020d0cf2daf09aa5d8b7fb4 occupancy f4e638427be784c018b314ae256b5722`.
- **Carved boxes join across rows.** `carve.emit` joined cells along x and z; it also joins finished boxes of neighboring rows that have the same span, the same heights and the same choice (`Closing` in `carve.d`), which can never make more boxes than before. The cells painted are the same: the terrain's and the vaults' digest lines did not move. Brushes within 2.5 units of the root: vaults 165895 to 103673, planet 280326 to 227881, caves 34301 to 26493. `bench-gen` on the vaults, the parameters: 254, 269 and 290 ms before, 221, 224 and 228 after; on the planet the same before and after. The planet's load from spawn holds 5.1 million brushes (258 MB) where it held 6.5 million (326 MB).
- **A tile's reach.** A risen ground's face tile was asked for by every node within 7 units of its plane, so most brushes a session held belonged to tiles 8 to 14 units from the camera, asked for from the sky over their ground. `api.tileReach(ground, above, below)`, in cells (plugin ABI 34; `above` and `below` on a `RisingKind`), says how far from its plane a ground's tile paints: only a node within that reach, or on the foot line, asks for the tile, and a tile that paints into a node past it is refused with a message. Only the world's own structure may call it, and not for an upright kind. The vaults give 22 cells above and 1 below. The backrooms give what their content comes to (`reachAbove` and `reachBelow` in `rooms/layout.d`): above, the top floor's chapel roof or its court tree, whichever is higher; below, the pit of a chapel on the lowest floor; the backrooms world gives it to its root and the shared kinds to every risen backrooms, and the backrooms' own digest lines are the same with it as without. The library gives 12 cells above and 12 below, the slab its face tile paints (`ROOF + 1` and `-BOTTOM`; its stacks are an upright kind and have none), and its own digest lines, `digest populated=10140 materials ccef4e12ec10a59ca14085e7da4c1409 occupancy d39a2181f43d799ab0bc8c0169bd2896` and `deep populated=856 materials 502fb97987a72589382a2144ba9c5da0 occupancy 36773442d51ac61f86f1139974344b1d`, are the same with it as without. The planet's load from spawn, three runs each: 4.77, 4.82 and 4.90 s (284 to 291 frames) with no reach, 4.25, 4.25 and 4.37 s (253 to 260 frames) with the vaults', 4.14, 4.21 and 4.24 s (246 to 252 frames) with the backrooms' as well, 4.02, 4.10 and 4.16 s (240 to 247 frames) with all three; 4.9 to 5.2 million brushes with none, 3.6 to 3.7 million with the vaults', 2.3 to 2.5 million with two, 1.85 to 1.88 million with three; 683 to 708 MB in use with none, 605 to 613 MB with the vaults', 515 to 522 MB with two, 477 to 479 MB with three. Those figures are for a reach taken exactly, and taken exactly it made flight worse: of the 1800 frames of the six legs, 51, 55 and 60 ran past 16.7 ms with no reach and 67, 67, 68 and 81 with the three, though the average step was the same or shorter. Switching the refusal off changed nothing, so the check is not what costs. What costs is that asking from far away had been a look-ahead: a tile was painted long before its ground came into the load reach, and with an exact reach it is painted when its ground arrives, in the same frames that need it. So a node asks from `REACH_AHEAD` (1.5 units) past the reach, and the refusal allows the same: 44, 55 and 63 late frames, level with no reach at all, for 2.2 to 2.3 million brushes and 498 to 502 MB in place of 1.85 million and 478 MB, and a load of 4.11, 4.16 and 4.19 s. The four `live/` tests ran in 3.6, 2.6, 30.2 and 24.6 s with the reaches against 4.4, 2.7, 34.6 and 25.7 s before the day's work (one run each). On the planet, `bench-gen` reads 230, 241 and 261 ms for the parameters where it read 286, 289 and 294, with 83157 brushes within 2.5 units of spawn where there were 227881, and `bench-drive` reads 186, 189 and 190 ms where it read 246 and 275. Law: `structure/overreach`, on a rig the caves plugin registers. The planet gives its wild ground, which is also its root, the reach of a lattice grove (`groveAbove` and `groveBelow` in `lattice.d`: an edge and the 9 cells a tree stands over a pole's end above, the footing's edge below). That one is small: about 75.8 thousand tile tasks in the load where there were 78.9 thousand, 20.0 to 20.3 thousand live tiles where there were 21.5 to 22.1 thousand, the same brushes, and times and late frames inside the noise (3.97 and 4.16 s, 63 late frames). Not built: a reach for the skies' grounds, which do not rise yet. What is left far from the camera is the city's. The city was given a reach and it was taken out again, because it changed nothing that was measured (4.30 s and 2.4 million brushes with it): its tallest building is 38 stories (`most-stories` in `blocks.content`) of 4 cells with a crown of up to 23 over it (a row house's hip roof on a lot joined on both low sides; a glass tower's masts on its penthouse come to 14), which is 7.8 units and past the 7 the engine asks from anyway, so a reach could only bound what lies under the city (a pond bed, 2 cells down), and that did not show in the load from spawn. It was exact: the city's own digest lines, `digest populated=10140 materials 1142103eb2e30d115b80a85ed48692e5 occupancy a612f7e1e8179d510c59f65447c034ff` and `deep populated=856 materials d55b793e9e22ec643cdde6eaefaa09e4 occupancy 2996b928096ebdbd19fce2b6ea1f24ae`, were the same with it. What would cut the city's far tiles is a reach per face, from how tall the buildings on that face are, which the tile only knows once it runs. A connector is painted by the risen ground's tile far below that ground and is let through because it lies on the foot line; the connector rigs pass with the backrooms' reach, which is the evidence that every style stays on that line. Under a root ground the reach saves the walk down to the ground and no tiles, since a node's ancestors are finished first and they include the ground under it.
- **The distance to a flattened line.** `MeasuredLine.distanceTo` took the inverse hyperbolic sine of each plane's product and then the sine of that; it uses the product itself (`sinhDistanceTo`). The two are the same number in exact arithmetic and can differ in the last place of a float, so this one rests on the digests and not on an argument: the terrain's, the vaults', the backrooms', the library's and the city's lines are all as they were. `bench-gen` on the planet, the voxels: 392, 404 and 414 ms before, 304, 305 and 306 after. The streaming load reads the same, since the main thread bounds it; this is time the workers get back.
- **A tile's strokes are walked where they are drawn.** For every stroke a tile drew (a limb, a clump of leaves), the main thread walked the honeycomb from the stroke's node to find the nodes it reaches, making those nodes as it went. The worker that paints the tile does that walk now (`StrokeWalk` in `strokes.d`, the same walk a placed structure's strokes still take on the main thread), and hands over each stroke with its node's id and the nodes it reaches; the main thread only records them. The lists of reached nodes are freed once a batch is applied, since each entry carries a double-precision motion. Planet digest, repaint and the tree and `live/` tests as they were. In the profile of the load the main thread took 2879 samples where it took 3050 to 3120, with applying painted tiles at 7.3 in 100 where it was 11.4; the load reads 4.00, 4.02 and 4.07 s (4.11 to 4.19 before), memory in use 494 to 509 MB, and flight's late frames 48 and 68 of 1800, inside the band they were in.
- **More painting ahead while the world first loads.** Painting tiles ahead of the scan is the parallel way to do work the scan otherwise does one node at a time on the main thread, and it was held to 2 ms and 256 candidate nodes a frame for the sake of smooth flight. While the first nearby set is still being built (`NearbyStage.initial`) it gets 4 ms and 512 (`LOADING_PAINT_MILLISECONDS` in `traversal.d`, `LOADING_CANDIDATES` in `world.d`; the allowance is a parameter of `paintAhead` and of `NearbyScratch.advance` now, not a constant inside the tile work), and afterwards exactly what it had. The planet's load from spawn: 3.62, 3.70 and 3.70 s (216 to 221 frames) where it was 4.00, 4.02 and 4.07 s; flight is as it was, 48 late frames of 1800 in the run read and no paint phase over 4 ms among its slow frames. With the larger allowance in flight as well, tried first, the load was the same but single paint phases ran to 17 and 18 ms. Nothing generated changes: digest, repaint and forget as they were.
- **The scan hands over what its workers found, a window at a time.** The streamer's scan judges each window of 1024 nodes on the workers, and to judge a node a worker works out its state and its horospheres on a view of its own. Those were merged into the graph once, after every window had been scanned, and a tile run in between threw them away (`ThreadViews.begin` forgets what each view holds), so the main thread worked the same things out again, a node at a time, as it scanned. Each window is merged as soon as its pass ends (`scanWindows` in `streaming.d`). With it goes a table that costs nothing to clear when it is empty: `HashTable.clear` wrote every control byte of the table whatever it held, a thread view's tables are cleared at the start of every parallel pass, and most are empty then. The two were measured together. The planet's load from spawn: 3.39, 3.39, 3.40 and 3.42 s (202 to 204 frames) where it was 3.62, 3.70 and 3.70 s; the thread views hold 28 to 36 MB where they held 63 to 71, and 473 to 482 MB are in use where there were 494 to 514. Flight's late frames are where they were: 71, 42, 42 and 55 of 1800. The four `live/` tests ran in 3.2, 2.6, 27.6 and 23.2 s against 3.4 to 3.6, 2.6, 29.5 to 30.2 and 24.0 to 24.6. Nothing generated changes: 132 tests, 138 views, 9 films, both digest lines, `check-repaint` on all ten worlds and `check-forget` as they were.
- **The bench counts flight's late frames, and no longer hides them.** `caapi --bench-load` ends its six legs with one line, `flight: N of 1800 frames over 16.7ms`, so a run is compared by one number and not by reading every leg. Every tenth frame of a leg the bench counts the chunks loaded around the camera, which took about a fifth of the main thread's time in flight, and that time was charged to the game: the step is paced by how much of the frame was left idle, so the count kept pulling the step's budget down. The game is paced before the count now (`paceFrame`, then the count, then the sleep). With the step at the budget it has in play, the same build reads 108, 125 and 109 late frames of 1800 where the bench had read 42 to 71, and an average step of 11.2 ms in the last leg where it read 10.2 to 10.5. Every count of late frames in this file from before this item was taken the old way and is lower than play; they compare with each other and not with the new ones. The load is not touched by this (3.33, 3.50 and 3.50 s in those three runs, with nothing in the load changed: the machine was noisier than in the runs above).
- **A late frame gives its whole shortfall back.** What the late frames are made of, timed inside the streamer over a run: a streamer phase of 9 ms or more is nearly all scan (planning the parameters of even 7 thousand chunks is 0.3 to 2 ms), most such scans had that long to run because painting and the nearby set took little, and they end 1 to 3 ms past their deadline on the one node being finished when it ran out, sometimes 5 to 8. After the step comes the render, whose chunk update meshes for up to 2 ms and uploads, 2.5 to 3 ms whenever meshes are pending and now and then 6 to 10. So a late frame is a full step, one heavy node and a render that grew. The step's budget follows the idle time of the frame before (`LocalWorld.paceBy`, 3 ms of idle wanted), and it gave back a quarter of a shortfall per frame, so after the render grew the next several frames ran as close to the edge as the one that was late. Once the world has settled for the first time (`Arrival.settled`) a frame with less idle than wanted gives the whole shortfall back at once (`BUDGET_LOSS`), and the budget grows back a quarter of the spare per frame as before; until then it is paced as it was, so the first load is not touched. Flight: 57, 66 and 58 late frames of 1800 where the same harness read 108, 125 and 109; the chunks within 4 units of the camera in the last leg 16.3 to 16.5 thousand on average and 16.1 to 16.3 thousand at least, the same either way; the load 3.43, 3.46 and 3.50 s. What it costs is far loading: the average step in the last leg is 10.7 to 11.0 ms where it was 11.2, and the world settles 64 to 70 frames after the flight stops where it took 51 to 55. Applied during the first load as well it made the load 3.71 s, which is why it waits. 132 tests as they were. Tried on the way and taken out: bounding each window's judging pass by the scan's deadline (nothing), stopping the scan as early as its heaviest recent node took (130 late frames and a slower load), and holding the step to 12 ms after the first nearby set (83 and 97 late frames, the same far backlog as the give-back for a smaller gain).
- **A node asks about its edges' grounds once, and reads a plane's height where it meant the height.** For the edge tile's ceiling, each of a node's 30 edges gathered the grounds of the node that owns the edge and measured every one of them again, about seven calls into the math library for each ground of each edge. A node's edges have only a handful of owners, the node and a few of its ancestors, so the grounds are gathered and measured once for each owner (`EdgeOwners` in `tiles.d`): the same arithmetic, so the same answers. And the distance from a plane to the center of its own node is the plane's stored height: `Plane.distanceTo(Point.origin())` worked that out again with six calls into the math library and came to the same float every time (compared on every call across the digest, the four `live/` tests, the library's tests and `falls/planet`, without one difference, before anything was changed), so the 21 places that asked it of the origin read `height`. The terrain's, the vaults', the backrooms', the library's and the city's digest lines, `check-repaint` on all ten worlds with the same tile counts, `check-forget`, 132 tests, 138 views, 9 films and `content-check`: as they were. In the profile of the load, finding a node's tiles is 7.2 and 4.3 in 100 of the main thread's time (its own fallback and its share of the workers' passes) where it was 9.6 and 4.7, and the main thread took 2367 and 2416 samples where it took 2502. The load reads 3.41, 3.13 and 3.19 s; flight 53, 63 and 50 late frames with the world settling 56 to 62 frames after it stops, which is most of the far loading the give-back cost; the four `live/` tests 3.1, 2.5, 26.1 and 22.9 s where they were 3.1 to 3.2, 2.6, 27.3 to 27.7 and 23.2 to 23.4. `bench-drive` reads 173, 196 and 198 ms. `bench-gen` reads 253 to 261 ms for the parameters and 350 to 358 for the voxels, and those cannot be set beside the morning's 230 and 305: the voxel code is as it was then, and the vaults' parameters, whose code has only got faster, read 267 ms now against 221 to 228 this morning, so that single-threaded bench runs about a sixth slower on this machine tonight than it did then. Against the voxels as a yardstick the parameters are lower than they were (0.72 of them against 0.75 at best).
- **A node's second plane.** Every node state carried two planes reflected from its parent: the ground, and a `reference` plane that only a tilted plane world reads (a chunk's level on a slope, and which way the player faces at spawn there; the compiler lists no other reader in the three programs). It is a `Maybe!Plane` now, made and carried only on tilted ground. The terrain's digest lines and the slope and flat tests are as they were; in the profile of the load, making node states is 9.9 in 100 of the workers' time where it was 12.0, and reflecting planes 9.2 where it was 11.6.
- **Tried and taken out,** each exact by the digest and each showing nothing in three runs: a radius check before the spire's `pow` and a shell check before the niche's cosine in the vaults' hall, remembering the last edge in `useEdges`, and running a batch of fewer than eight tile tasks on the calling thread (the pool's jobs are mostly scan windows, not small tile batches: 7711 jobs with it, about 7900 without). Two more on the workers: keeping each cell's depth for `riserAt` (exact, 299 to 301 ms of voxels against 304 to 306, not worth a second cache in the generator), and visiting each of the 63 nodes around a node once in `reliefAround` where it visits them 160 times (the same span everywhere across the `live/` tests, compared side by side, and no cheaper: the cost is making the states of nodes that do not exist yet, which happens once either way). Three more on the main thread, each leaving the planet's digest and repaint as they were. Painting a scanned node's ancestors ahead: a node is finished after its ancestors, and each ancestor that nobody painted ahead has its tiles found and run on the main thread, which counted out as 39 thousand of the load's 77 thousand tiles, 34 thousand of them for ancestors; planning those ancestors on the workers with the candidates left 7 to 9 thousand tiles to the main thread and the load came to 3.31, 3.32 and 3.34 s against 3.39, 3.39 and 3.40 without, with late frames no different (55, 44, 55 against 71, 42, 42), but it painted about 7 thousand tiles more (ancestors of nodes that were painted ahead and then never scanned) and held about 25 MB more, which is too little gained for a second way to pick what is painted. Painting ahead after the nearby set is refreshed in place of before it: the same counts, and 61 and 70 late frames, because the nearby phase can no longer leave room for a paint phase it has not seen. Keeping a node's proof of open sky when the scan has to ask its depth first (`scanAt` drops it, so such a node's chunks are planned and found empty in place of being filled as air): fewer chunks planned and 3.39, 3.42 and 3.60 s, nothing. What the counting showed is left: the scan still reaches about 25 thousand nodes in a load that nobody painted ahead, nearly all with nothing to paint, and a deep node inside the deep reach is never painted ahead though each has about twenty cave tiles; Painting from inside the scan was then tried for the first of these: every node of a judged window that the scan is about to finish and nobody has painted goes to the painter in one pass before the window is scanned. It read as a faster load, 3.27, 3.28 and 3.30 s, and 71, 73 and 79 late frames in flight, and both came from the same place: the pass ran at the end of the step whether or not the scan had time left. Held to the scan's deadline it gave nothing (3.42 s), because once a window is merged a node with nothing to paint costs the main thread very little. So a shorter load is only a gain when the step is no longer and the late frames are no more. And three on the worker pool, where the main thread waits about a quarter of each step for workers to finish what they took (650 to 750 ms of the load, 3 ms a frame in flight): painting the nearby set's frontier in batches of 256 nodes where it takes 64 made fewer passes (5424 where there were 6400) with the same wait and the same load (3.43 s); spinning a tenth of a millisecond before the main thread sleeps on a pass's last workers (756 ms waited, 3.49 s); and an idle worker lingering a fifth of a millisecond for the next pass before it sleeps (3.53 s). So the wait is the workers' own items still running, a candidate's burial proof or a heavy tile, not the cost of waking anyone.
- **Where the main thread's time goes now,** from a profile of the load with the reaches in: 3122 samples of its CPU where there were 3702; tile planning 15 in 100, finding the tiles of a node as it is scanned 14, horosphere discovery 14 (it overlaps both), applying painted tiles 11 of which adding strokes 5, and about 0.8 s of the 4.2 s spent waiting for the workers to finish some 7900 parallel jobs. At the end of the day, from a profile of a load of 3.4 s: 2416 samples; planning tiles on the workers' passes 17 in 100, running them 18 of which applying what was painted 9, finding a node's tiles as it is scanned 12, horosphere discovery 11 (it overlaps all three), carrying planes from node to node 8, and still about 0.8 s waiting on some 7200 parallel jobs.
- **N3, half of it:** the view `backroomswellbelow` stands in the backrooms' well one level down; rendered and looked at. The lattice joints in deep cave floors still have no view.
- **Two views that do not show their subject,** rendered and looked at, neither changed: `bigtree` backs a unit out of spawn into a lattice grove, whose steel pole fills the frame, and from 2.4 units back the grove is all there is in the frame and no tree; `trees` ends with the camera against oak wood. The world they look at is as it was (the digest), so both were like this before today.

Built on 2026-11-14, outside the packages, and checked by section 4 (132 tests, 138 views, 9 films, `content-check` 0 problems, the digest lines of the terrain, the vaults, the backrooms, the library and the city as they were, `check-repaint` with `differing=0` and the same tile counts on all ten worlds, `check-forget` on lattice, all three programs built from the one tree):

- **A chunk that waited for a neighbor is meshed.** A chunk beside one that is being generated, or whose voxels were dropped as far away, is not meshed: it waits and is asked again. It was asked again through `markChanged`, which queues only a chunk that `needsMesh`, and a chunk of one material (open air) needs a mesh only while a populated neighbor shows against it. So an air chunk queued beside its one solid neighbor fell out of the queue if that neighbor's voxels were dropped before the air chunk's turn came (the mesher has 2 ms a frame, and the evictor's sweep drops a far chunk once it is meshed itself and its neighbors have settled). The neighbor was then restored for it, a restored chunk queues nobody, and the air chunk went on drawing nothing where it should draw the neighbor's faces at their border (across a node face each side draws the other side's faces). It needs meshing to run behind generation. The city's runs about 15 frames behind in the load, and one of the handful of city loads read ended with three such chunks; the planet's keeps up, and no planet load showed it. With the mesher held to 0.1 ms a frame the planet shows it at once: 8 chunks drawing no faces at the end of the load, 204 faces short of what the same run ends with once fixed, and more after the flight. `MeshBatch.requeueWaiting` puts a chunk that waited back in the queue whatever it holds. The held-down run then ends its load and its flight with no chunk short of faces.
- **The bench checks the meshes after the flight as well, and says which way they are off.** `caapi --bench-load` compares every mesh with a fresh one once the load has settled and again once the flight has, and the line reads `meshes checked=N stale=M, of which K draw fewer faces than they should`. K is the one that has to be 0. After a flight M is a couple of hundred (182 to 220 in the runs read) and every one of them draws more than a fresh mesh would: a chunk keeps the faces it drew against a neighbor whose node has since been unloaded, and is meshed again when that node comes back. The count of faces at the end of a load is not a check by itself: the planet's load ends with 2934967 faces or with 14 fewer, and no chunk short either way. The loads that differ have the same nodes holding chunks and the same nodes counted as deep in every shell, and differ by a handful of faces in the shells 4 to 6 units out (19 fewer in one, 5 more in the next), with the scan's painter or without it. A node proven buried before the scan first reaches it is loaded only within the deep reach, one proven later has already been loaded as far as everything else, and which of the two happens depends on what the workers could see at the time; that this is where the faces go is inferred from where they differ and was not looked at directly.
- **Once settled, a step is at most 12 ms.** The step's budget may grow to 13 ms until the world has first settled and to 12 after (`MOST_SETTLED_STEP_BUDGET` in `world.d`), on top of the give-back: a full step, one heavy node and a render that grew fit the frame more often. Planet flight: 48, 47 and 50 late frames of 1800 where the give-back alone read 53, 63 and 50, the world settling 56 to 62 frames after the flight stops as before, the load 3.26 to 3.29 s and not touched, since the ceiling waits for the first settle. City flight: 36 and 43 where it read 48. Those runs were taken one tree after the other. Measured again back to back on the tree of 2026-11-14, with the ceiling, without it twice, and with it again: 31, then 38 and 43, then 31 late frames, the world settling 44 and 53 frames after the flight with it and 44 and 62 without.
- **The scan paints ahead of itself.** The city's late frames come in bursts when a new nearby set is published: the scan starts over on a set full of nodes nobody painted and spends its whole step finding and running their tiles a node at a time, though the average step is 2.6 ms, and three of every five city tiles were painted that way. At a node it is about to finish that nobody has painted, the scan hands that node and the unpainted ones after it in the judged window, up to 256, to the painter in one parallel pass of at most 2 ms, and only while the step has that long left (`Streamer.paintFrom`). Measured with it switched off and on in builds run back to back, the mesh pass of the next item in both. City flight: 0 and 1 late frames of 1800 with it and 5 without; the average step in each of the six legs 1.81 to 2.20 ms with it and 2.13 to 2.56 without; one tile in six painted when needed where it is three in five; the load 1.27 to 1.35 s either way. Planet: nothing in the late frames (28, 36, 35 and 28 with it, 36 and 27 without), the load 3.12 to 3.27 s with it and 3.24 and 3.30 without, 137 to 143 thousand tiles painted when needed over a run where there are 192 thousand without. Headless: `live/far` 24.8 s with it and 26.2 without, the other three `live/` tests and the library's two the same (3.0, 2.5 to 2.6, 22.9, 16.8 to 16.9, 3.8 to 3.9), `bench-drive` the same (194 and 190 ms with it, 196 and 195 without). The first measurement of it read `bench-drive` as 177 ms against 197 and the city's late frames as 23 against 36 and 43; those pairs were taken at different times of the day and were the machine, not the change. The pass tried the day before went once for a whole window before any of it was scanned, and held to the deadline it gave the planet's load nothing; this one is small on the planet too, and what it buys is the city's flight and less tile work on the main thread.
- **A step that runs over takes it out of the mesh pass.** What was left of the late frames was mostly a step of 13.5 to 14.7 ms, which is the budget and the one node being finished when it ran out, followed by a render of 3 ms, 2 of them the mesh pass, which took its 2 ms whatever the step had done. Once the world has first settled, what a step runs past 12 ms (`LocalWorld.stepOverrun`) comes out of that frame's mesh pass, down to a quarter of a millisecond (`LEAST_MESH_BUDGET_MILLISECONDS` in `renderer.d`), so the meshes of such a frame wait one frame. Planet flight: 27, 29 and 26 late frames of 1800 where the tree just before read 51, 49 and 57; the world settles 53, 39 and 42 frames after the flight where it took 63, 50 and 93; the same chunks loaded around the camera (16221 to 16225 within 4 units in the last leg either way); the load 3.34, 3.14 and 3.16 s against 3.13, 3.19 and 3.18, and it is not touched, since nothing runs over before the first settle is counted. City flight: 1 late frame of 1800 where it read 23 and 22, and that one is a render of 14.6 ms in which the face buffer grew; it settles 14 frames after the flight, as before. With the scan's painter off as well the city reads 5, where it read 36 to 48 before either. The planet's count drifts with the machine: later the same day the same tree read 28 to 36.
- **The count of late frames drifts within a day, so it is compared back to back.** One tree read 23, 24 and 25 late frames of 1800 in one half hour and 35 and 28 in the next; `bench-drive` read 177 ms in the morning and 190 to 200 ms in the afternoon with the code that matters to it unchanged. A change to pacing is measured with a build that has it and a build that does not, run back to back, at least two runs of each; runs of one tree set beside runs of another from a different hour are not a comparison. Two things in this file were first recorded from runs that were not alternated and were corrected when they were: the scan's painter above, and everything in the next item.
- **What a slow step is made of, and four things that did not help.** Timed inside the streamer over whole runs (the probes are out again): a streamer phase past 12 ms happens 15 to 22 times in a run. Most are the scan using the whole step. A few are building plans handed to the workers after the scan's deadline with no bound, 5 to 9 ms for up to 155 of them. A few are one pass over the arrivals taking 6 ms where the same pass over as many arrivals (30 to 40 thousand chunks of open air in one step) takes half a millisecond, which is a worker losing its core in the middle of the pass and not work. A single node that takes the scan over 4 ms is rare, 30 in a run, every one a node whose tiles nobody painted, and for most of them the painting made 300 to 1700 new nodes on the main thread. A paint pass ran more than 2 ms past its allowance 346 times in a run: the pass is bounded by a count of tasks, not by the clock, and planning takes up to 6.7 ms of it in up to four rounds for as few as 64 candidates, most of which have nothing to paint; then the run waits for its slowest tile (about 2 ms) and the main thread applies what was painted (up to 2 ms). Tried against these, each built and checked, and each taken out because the late frames, the loads and the walls of the streaming tests did not move by more than they move by themselves (what each was measured against is in its brackets, and only the bound on building plans was run back to back): stopping the scan at an unpainted node when the step has no time left for a pass, so that the next frame's first pass starts there (without the clock-bounded passes it read 36 late frames in the one run taken, and that first pass then has more to do; by itself it made the `live/` flights 0.8 s shorter in the one run taken); bounding a pass by the clock, half the allowance for planning with a measured cost per candidate, a deadline on the run, and a candidate counted as covered only if all its tiles ran (slow passes 182 where there were 346 and none over 5.7 ms where they reached 10, and with the scan's stop 29, 21 and 32 late frames in the stretch after one that read 26 to 31 without either); bounding the building plans handed over in one step, to 1.5 ms or to 1.5 ms past the step's deadline (29, 21 and 32 without it and 18, 19 and 27 with it in one stretch, then 28 and 36 without and 35 and 28 with when run back to back; bounded from the first frame it made the city's load settle 20 frames later, and bounded always it cost the library's flight test 0.2 s); and asking a chunk with unresolved materials about the one face an arrival is on in place of all six (the terrain's and four other worlds' digests the same; the pass it was aimed at turned out to be the stall above). What is left in a pass is a straggler in each part, and what is left in a step is work the main thread waits for; both want a painter the main thread does not wait on, which is a larger change than any of these.
- **A risen ground out of reach is told from its foot.** In flight the workers are mostly idle (about two cores' worth of work) and the main thread waits on its parallel passes, so what a pass costs is what counts, and a profile put one thing at the top on both sides: finding the risen grounds near a node for its burial proof (`addRisingOver`, 15 in 100 of the workers' time and 8.7 of the main thread's). Counted over a run: 9.1 million times a node asks whether a risen ground near its ground node is within reach, 6.7 million of them answered by carrying the ground's planes from its foot along a path of 8.6 faces on average (57 million plane reflections, each with its square roots and an `asinh`), and 1.4 million of all the questions answered yes. A point costs far less to carry than a plane, so the node's center is carried back along the path to the foot in double precision first (`centerAlong`), and the planes are carried only if the center is not beyond the reach by `REACH_MARGIN`, a quarter of a unit (`isPlaneBeyond`, `isLineBeyond` in `horospheres.d`). The carry itself is untouched, so whatever is within reach gets the plane it always got. A build that did both and halted on any disagreement ran the bench (6.2 million carries, 6.1 million of which the filter skips), the five worlds' digests, the planet's repaint, the lattice's forget and 31 tests across risen grounds without one. Headless, with the filter, without it, and with it again: the library's flight 3.3, 3.9 and 3.3 s (its stacks are upright grounds, which a node is almost never within reach of); `live/far` 24.4, 24.7 and 23.9 s; `live/fly` 22.4, 22.8 and 22.6 s; `bench-drive` 186 and 190, then 193 and 200, then 192 ms; `bench-gen` parameters 263, 270 and 261 ms beside voxels of 373, 361 and 357. In the profile of a flight the workers took 13849 samples where they took 15222, with `refWithinReach` at 1.9 in 100 where it was 5.1, and the main thread 18716 where it took 19120. What is left of the discovery, by the workers' share: listing what rises near a ground node 6.4, walking down to the ground node 5.0 (for a risen ground every step of the walk carries that ground's plane from its foot again), finding the path 2.4.
- **Tiles are planned while the frame waits for the display.** A frame is paced: after the render the main thread waits for the display, the workers have nothing to do, and the next step then opens with planning passes it waits on. The pool can be handed a job and joined later (`WorkerPool.start` and `join` in `parallel.d`; `run` is the two together, and with `start` only the workers take items). After its chunk update the renderer calls `LocalWorld.planAhead`, which gathers what the next step would plan first, the next batch of unknown nodes of the nearby set being built (`NearbyScratch.collectUnpainted`) and the frame-start candidates, and starts their planning on the workers (`Grower.startAhead`, `TileWork.startAhead`). `LocalWorld.joinAhead` ends it (`Grower.finishAhead`): the main thread joins the job, merges the views, takes the plans up to the task budget, runs the tiles and marks the nodes covered. It is called before anything changes the graph: at the top of `Game.step`, in the play loop after the wait for the display, at the head of `update` and in `release`. Its time is charged to the step and to the paint phase, and a step that finished such a pass skips its own frame-start pass. Between the two calls nothing may change the graph or begin another pass on the thread views; a second render without a step in between starts nothing (`Ahead`), and what renders outside a frame loop joins before it hands back (`SettledFrame.render`, for screenshots and films, whose scripts move the body between frames without a step). The start is in the renderer and not in the loops because of where the wait is: with a driver that cannot wait for a present `waitForDisplay` does nothing, and the frame is then held inside the acquire or the present, which come after the chunk update (read in `renderer.d`; no such driver was run). The planning is the same planning, so the worlds are as they were (the list at the head of this block was run on this tree). Planet, in builds run back to back, with it, without it, with it, without it: the load 2.99 and 3.06 s with it, 3.07 and 3.11 without; over the flight the nearby phase 3.16 and 3.11 ms a frame against 3.81 and 3.72 (6103 and 6357 ms of nearby time against 6995 and 6843), the paint phase 0.80 and 0.82 against 1.15 and 1.20, the streamer 4.09 and 4.16 against 3.65 and 3.84, since what the first two give up the scan takes; 63137 and 64066 parallel jobs against 66482 and 66373, the main thread waiting 5042 and 5030 ms on them against 5538 and 5579; the average step of the last leg 9.42 and 9.48 ms against 10.00 and 10.10 (7.01 and 7.11 of main thread CPU against 7.35 and 7.44); 29 and 28 late frames of 1800 against 34 and 38; the world settling 49 and 48 frames after the flight against 55 and 55, and a rebuild of the nearby set pending 16 and 14 frames against 20 and 19. With only the frame-start candidates planned in the wait and not the nearby set's batch (one run): the paint phase 0.58, the nearby phase 3.58, the last leg's step 9.67 ms, 24 late frames, the load 3.14 s. The tree as it stands, one run each: planet load 3.00 s, 27 late frames, settled 41 frames after the flight, no mesh short of faces; city load 1.26 s, 1 late frame. What the join still does on the main thread is run the planned tiles and apply them, and only one pass is planned per frame. Not exercised: the play loop with a real display and input, and leaving a world while a pass is started; the bench, two screenshots and the film `pan` (30 frames, the last one looked at) are what ran.
- **What the main thread waits for, and whose tiles those are.** Measured with probes in the pool and around each tile, which are out again; nothing was changed for it. In flight the main thread waits 4.3 s of 1800 frames for workers to finish the parallel passes it opened, 2.4 ms of a step of 9.3, over about 31 passes a frame. By the pass's kind, the flight alone: running planned tiles 12141 passes of 43 tiles each, 2479 ms waited (0.2 ms a pass, against 0.08 ms of the main thread's own work in it); planning candidates 21701 passes of 59 nodes, 1243 ms; the scan's judging of its windows 9988 passes, 287 ms; meshing 1722 passes, 106 ms; settling arrivals 84 ms; everything else under 60 ms together. So seven parts in eight of the wait is tile work, and two thirds of that is waiting for the slowest tile of a pass. The tiles, timed one by one over a load and a flight (about 26 s of worker time, 33 s on the clock): the vaults' `hall` 16502 tiles for 10.7 s, 0.65 ms each and not one under 0.2 ms; the planet's edge tile 473 thousand for 9.8 s, of which 2500 take 2.6 s between them (1 ms each, the ones that carve) and the rest 15 us each; two kinds the `horospheres` plugin registers 26013 tiles for 1.7 s and 8428 for 0.7 s (4.9 and 3.8 million brushes); the arbor's tile 809 for 0.9 s and a function of the `horospheres` plugin under the same tag 1054 for 1.2 s, 1.15 ms each (that one hands over no brushes; strokes were not counted); the planet's ground tile 57813 for 0.9 s; the library's 7834 for 0.3 s. In the profile of the flight `hall` is 8.7 in 100 of the workers' time: `cellChoice` 6.0, the carve helper's own loop 1.9, and the engine's paint calls for every tile there is 1.3. So a hall's cost is the plugin asking `cellChoice` about 16560 cells, five quarters of 12 by 12 by 23, and not the engine recording what comes out. Not built, in the order the numbers suggest: a cheaper `hall` (the earlier attempt on its `pow` and cosine showed nothing in `bench-gen`; this probe would show it directly, as milliseconds a tile); the same look at the carving edge tiles and the arbor; and a tile run the main thread does not wait on, which the idle-time planning is the first half of.
- **Tried and taken out, or weighed and not built.** Leaving nodes that are not waiting to be scanned out of the candidates of the frame's first paint pass: the same counts either way (398 thousand tiles ahead, 193 thousand when needed). Dropping a far tile's brushes as soon as it is painted would give back about 100 MB, but a tile asked for from the sky over its ground is a look-ahead, and nearly every ground tile on a flight's path would be painted twice. A reach for each face of the city, from how tall its buildings are: the heights come out of each tile's own run of random numbers, so there is no cheap bound that is exact. The streaming tests were run three times over on one tree to see whether they hold steady: 6 passed each time, `live/far` in 26.0 to 26.2 s.

After the owner played that build on 2026-11-14 and reported five things: something floats and sometimes sits over things; the picture is jittery; the structures are too square, hard to get into and ugly, the tree-like connector most of all; the big structures with nothing to do are bad looking, uninteresting and under-complex; cave entrances are hard to find and often go nowhere. Checked by section 4 on the tree as it stands (132 tests, 152 views, 9 films, `content-check` 0 problems, `check-repaint` with `differing=0` on all ten worlds, `check-forget` on lattice, all three programs built).

- **The first, second half of the third and fourth were one thing: the lattice grove.** The planet put a grove on 15 faces in 100 of wild ground. Its hut (plank walls, a roof slab, treads above) is painted at the ground plane whatever the terrain does, so it floats over a dip; a grove asked only whether a ground rises at its corners and not whether a sinkhole lies there, so it could stand on one. It is off the surface: `lattice.grove`, its footing and its odds are deleted, the planet's wild ground runs the shared wild tile and gives it a reach of nothing off the foot line. The lattice stays where it belongs, hanging under the crust. The planet's load holds 2.52 million faces where it held 2.93 million, 1943 buildings (28 MB) where there were 4465 (81 MB), and about 406 MB in use where it was about 480.
- **The existing views looked at the sky.** `connectortrunk`, `grove`, `risenwild` and the four `around` views rise 11 cells or more and tilt up, so what stands on the ground was never in them. `standing0` to `standing270` stand where a player spawns and look level, and `trunkfoot` stands at a trunk connector's door; all were rendered and looked at.
- **Once settled, a step is at most 8 ms.** A late frame is a hitch the player sees, because the frame after it moves twice as far. Steps run 4 to 8 ms past their budget on one heavy pass, so a ceiling of 12 ms left a 60 Hz frame no room. With 8 (`MOST_SETTLED_STEP_BUDGET`) and the grove gone, measured together and not apart, in two flights: 3 late frames of 1800 with the caves and connectors still as they were (load 2.85 s, the far world settling 68 frames after the flight where it took 41 to 57), 3 of 1800 with the trunk connector and three sinkholes in 100, the profiler on (load 3.07 s), and 2 of 1800 on the tree as it stands with the cave network, the profiler on (load 3.30 s), where the day's runs before read 18 to 39; the chunks within 4 units of the camera as they were (16.0 to 16.6 thousand a leg). The tree as it stands was not run without the profiler. What it costs beyond the slower far settle: a step that runs past 8 ms now takes its overrun out of the mesh pass, where it was past 12, so meshes wait a frame more often. That is the owner's answer to question 16, taken as: smoothness first. Not known: whether this is the jitter the owner saw, which could as well be the display's rate (the pacing assumes 60 Hz and its least step of 4 ms does not fit a 144 Hz frame) or something in the picture and not in the timing.
- **The caves are a network below the first layer of cells, with a way down beside every landmark.** Counted by floor material within 4 units of spawn, the world held one cave of two chambers, the one the world guarantees: a sinkhole was rolled at 2 vertices in 100 and nothing asked what lay under it, so a rolled one could be a bare pit. The owner, shown what more sinkholes cost, asked for more caves, a network of them, and obvious ways in. What a cave costs depends on where it is: a chamber within about 1.9 edges of the ground lies in the cells that touch the ground plane, which the engine cannot prove buried, so it is carved, built and meshed out to the full 5.9 units from above; below that the cells are buried and load only within 2.6. So: under open ground a vertex two edges down or deeper is a hall on its own at 3 in 10 where the rock holds it (`hall-odds`, `HALL_DEPTH`, `isHall`); from 1.6 edges down a chamber is open if any walkable neighbor above it is, and is joined to every open one (`waysUpOf`, `opennessOf`), so the trees under different sinkholes and the halls run into one another, under built grounds as well; a sinkhole is rolled at 6 in 100 and exists only where the vertex under it holds a chamber; the world forces one beside each of the twelve landmarks around spawn as well as the one near spawn (`Root.forced`, `Placed` in the planet), flattening the ground there; and every standing stone at a mouth has a lit top, the tall one two cells of it. Within 4 units of spawn the first floors come to 1443 cells where they were 398, and the floors below come to 51616 of silt, 30225 of silty loam and 56660 of lava where there were 146, none and none. Two faults it turned up and fixed: with two ramps coming into a chamber the rock under one plugged the other (the wedge now leaves another ramp's passage alone, `ramped` in `spaceAt`), and the walk around the foot of the entry stair crossed the sunken mouth of another tunnel (the route takes the tunnel nearest the last tread, `sweepTo`). Where the time of a cave tile goes was read from a profile and not from `bench-gen`, which does not time tiles and had said a cell bound was worth nothing: the per-cell choice was 20 in 100 of the workers' time in a load with the network, 15 of it the space test. A cell outside the chamber's own cylinder and away from every tunnel is now left alone without being evaluated (`isBeyond`), exactly: both digest lines of the planet and of the caves and every material count within 4 units were the same with it as without. With it the load reads 3.70 s where it read 4.34 (profiler on, halls from 1.6 edges), and with the halls moved under the first layer 3.24, then 3.30 with the landmark mouths. On the tree as it stands: `live/far` 27.3 s and `live/fly` 23.2 (25.1 and 22.9 before the day's work; 24.4 and 22.5 on this tree with 2 sinkholes in 100 and no network), `live/drift` 3.0 and `live/edit-kept` 2.5; the bench with the profiler on, load 3.30 s (3.07 before the network), 2 late frames of 1800 and no mesh short of faces, the main thread waiting 7.1 s on its parallel passes over the run where it waited 5.0, and the far world a little behind at the end of the last leg (reach 5.65 of 5.93). That is the price of the network and of twice the mouths, and it is paid on the far world, not in late frames. What a sinkhole alone costs, from the earlier tree: `live/far` 26.4 s at 5 in 100, 28.3 at 10. Near spawn the first floors still end where the thin crust does; a chamber that breaks through the crust into the void is package B.
- **The trunk connector is a tree, and a connector on rough ground can be walked into.** The trunk was an 8 cell square with slits and three square slabs of leaves. Its cross-section is rounded (`rounded` in `connector.d`: three boxes that make an octagon), its crown is six rounded tiers up to 22 cells across, its flare is rounded, and it has holes on two sides only. The owner looked again and called it undecorated, so it has dark grooves down its four corners, a whorl of four boughs every 16 cells up the trunk, each a limb with a cluster of leaves and none within 9 cells of either ground so no door is behind one, and a lamp over the lower door (`grooves`, `boughs`, `lamp`; `knot` in the connector's materials). A ground says whether it is rough (`relief` on a `connector-ground`; the wild ground is): there a connector cuts six rising steps out from each door through land that stands higher than its floor. It no longer fills under its floor or lays steps downward: those paint below the lower ground's plane, into nodes that never ask for the risen ground's tile, and now and then one was already built, which stops the world ("paints ground that is already built"; the streaming tests caught it, and it was also the 0.3 s `live/edit-kept` had gained). A connector over a dip therefore still hangs; the footing belongs to the lower ground's own tile, which is not written. The steps stop six cells out because a tile may not paint past its column.
- **Looked at from where a player stands, one thing at a time.** Views that stand on the ground and look level were added for each (`spiralfoot`, `pairedfoot`, `driftfoot`, `lanternfoot`, `citystreet`, `vaultshall`, `towerfoot`, `hamletfoot`, `wildhut`, beside `standing0` to `standing270`, `trunkfoot` and `trunkaside`), rendered and looked at. What they showed: the city street and the vaults' hall are detailed and were left alone; the lantern connector reads as a lantern; the spiral connector was four bare posts around a stair; the landmarks built from the shared box (`tower`, `crown`) were banded concrete boxes with slits; and the trees at spawn are ordinary one-block-trunk trees seen by a body under two blocks tall, not a coarse grid blown up, which an earlier note here had wrongly said (a tree has no scale: `Tree` in `palette.d`).
- **The box is dressed.** Every box tower has a sill under each window, a ledge under its battlements and a pinnacle on each corner (`sills`, `ledge`, `pinnacles` in `box.d`); the tower landmark also has corner buttresses to half its height and a plinth (`buttresses`); a hut has a stepped roof with eaves in place of battlements, a workbench and a lamp (`roof`, and `bench` and `lamp` in the box's palettes). The spiral connector has a ring of trim every second story. The 21 tests that walk these structures pass.
- **Shelters stand on wild ground where the groves were.** A timber shelter with a doorway on each side, sills, a roof with eaves, four workbenches as a table, a lamp, and steps down to the land on every side, at 12 ground vertices in 100 where the land lies between 6 cells below the plane and 2 above, no ground rises, no cave mouth is rolled and no landmark is near (`shelterAlong`, `probeShelter` in the planet). It is painted by the planet's edge tile, along the edge that stands on the vertex and the one that hangs under it, which is why it is the same from all four sides. It was first a hut painted by the wild ground's face tile, and that stopped the world twice over: a hut centred on a vertex spreads into the four faces around it and only one face's tile paints it, so a neighbouring node can already be built; and any reach given to the wild ground's tile other than none, or a reach given to `KindTag.init`, also ends in "paints ground that is already built" on risen grounds within a second of flight. The second was not traced: `tileReach` with `KindTag.init` does not seem to name the root alone. What the shelters cost at 25 in 100 was 1.5 s on `live/far`; at 12 the four `live/` tests read 3.2, 2.7, 27.3 and 23.4 s (3.0, 2.5, 27.3 and 23.2 without them), and the bench with the profiler on loads in 3.36 s with 7 late frames of 1800.
- **The digest lines now.** The planet: `digest populated=10140 materials 4a1e712be5c382634f290167f98cfba2 occupancy e1a3ccb492f10ddb61f1738d9f053b31` and `deep populated=856 materials 617ce220300dbf2e2b8e1780fb85abd7 occupancy c5ce402ac516716fd78a55a4b07a75c2`. The caves: `digest populated=10140 materials 6fd368ad803d597672557e6884f62f55 occupancy db471c611c7639d5700f645e0bdfd02b` and `deep populated=856 materials d781372a05fab52fcef1f72e147c86a2 occupancy cb04b6c49dd13a657348d553c4d816c1`. The first lines of the built grounds moved with the caves under them and their `deep` lines did not: the city `82a4dc02f510eb1b824c34768f5536c9` and `3ae548f2b5f27b14b324cbf456a88a0a`, the vaults `c7635358fadad66cbb5e6b6ff5ece9a0` and `4d96ae9c0b87dfb2378878d9862fc6ba`, the backrooms `261e2669b76e3f409a1cd3d0b36c7ef5` and `c326ad6fa9f2d7e280a818b57d719afe`. The library, which has no caves, is as it was. The tiles a repaint counts: planet 88798, city 3037, backrooms 3033, library 2554, vaults 3038, caves 29646, lattice 715410, the three skies 1371 each.
- **Open from the same report.** The tower, drift and hidden connector styles are still 8 cell square towers, and the palace, Babel, the road and the trail were not looked at. The tall trunk the owner photographed was not rendered again: the view `trunkfoot` is a short connector. The views `towerfoot` and `hamletfoot` stand too far back to judge detail by. Nothing was tried at the main thread's wait itself. The main thread's wait on straggling tiles is what the network costs and what the next entrance would cost; a tile run the main thread does not wait on is still not built.

Built on 2026-11-14, the owner having asked for every package that needs no further answer to be built as far as it can be. Checked by section 4 on the tree as it stands: 136 tests, 159 views, 9 films, `content-check` 0 problems, `check-repaint` with `differing=0` on all ten worlds (planet 71958 tiles, city 3037, backrooms 3061, library 2554, vaults 3038, caves 29646, lattice 715410, the skies 1371 each), `check-forget` on lattice, all three programs built. Every world's first digest line moved on purpose; the library's moved in materials and not in occupancy, which fits the sealed cave themes (inferred, not traced). The planet's load reads 3.60 s with the profiler on where it read 3.36 at the start of the round, with 5 late frames of 1800; the four `live/` tests 3.1, 2.6, 27.6 and 23.7 s against 3.2, 2.7, 27.3 and 23.4. The load is a quarter of a second slower and that is a failure by the rule of section 4: what costs it was not measured piece by piece.

- **E, the hidden stair, built.** A ground's `bridge` is `built`, `hidden` or `none`; a stair to a `hidden` ground is always the hidden style, nothing rises from a ground that is not `built`, the hidden style's aprons are its own floor, and the vaults are `hidden`. The vaults' tile calls the connector at its foot after painting its hall (painting it first let the hall paint over the stair). The foot test moved to the connector (`probeOwnsFoot`), and every ground's tile asks it there. Rig `risenvaults` in the vaults plugin. Laws: `structure/hiddenvaults` (nothing plain at the foot; without mist sight the body does not leave the ground) and `structure/hiddenvaultsclimb` (with it, the body climbs onto the vaults). Views `vaultsstair` and `vaultsstairmist`, rendered and looked at. The streaming tests ran six times without the old fault ("paints ground that is already built", four in six before).
- **B, drains, built; mouths and the forced route not.** Under open ground, a vertical edge whose upper end is in an open cave network and whose lower end is below the underside is a drain at one in two: half a wooden spiral stair hanging out of the rock into the void (`stairDown` in the drain material), half a bare chute (`drain` in `caves.d`, `drain-odds` and `drain-stair-odds`). Seen once in a render with the openness test switched off; with it on, no drain stands within eight hops of the void under spawn, because the crust there is two edges and the network starts at 1.6, so no open chamber lies within reach of the underside. No law: nothing near spawn to state it about.
- **F, the void in layers, built in the planet; G1, G2 and G5 built.** The planet hands each edge in the void to a layer by how far below the underside its lower end is: the lattice down to `lattice-layer` (3 edges, the boundary moved by up to half an edge by the edge's hash), floating islands below that (`voids` in the planet). The registry of void worlds and per-ground content layers of the design are not built; the layering lives in the planet's settings. The lattice has weaves in content (`lattice-weave`: beam width, kept share, broken and doubled beams, platform and stair odds), chosen per region; one weave, `regular`, has no randomness (a stair on every pole, a doubled girder on every edge of one direction) and breaks one region and one edge in 81 (G2). The lattice world on its own uses the plain weave, which is the old lattice. Islands (G5): one ground vertex in 25 in the island layer holds a turf island with a tree, painted wholly inside the edge below it; two islands at the ends of a level edge are joined by a plank bridge. Law: `structure/void` now says the body lands on the lattice or an island (it lands on an island). Views `void`, `island`, rendered and looked at.
- **H1, H2, H3, H4 (first half), built.** H1: a service tunnel under every city street, a wet concrete bore with ceiling lamps and a steel pipe, open where tunnels meet, and a stair down from the sidewalk on one street in four (`tunnel` in `blocks.d`). Law `structure/citytunnel`, view `citytunnel`. H2: a fourth backrooms level, `rooms:works` (boiler, pipeworks, cistern), content only; view `backroomsworks`. H3: one vault hall in three has a crypt under it, a chamber with niches in the hall's palette reached by a stair through the floor; the vaults' tile reach below is now nine cells. View `vaultscrypt`. H4: a sealed cave takes `caves:sealed-<theme>`, with more light and more hidden-family stone. The richer air of a sealed cave is not built: it needs `air.d` read and designed.
- **L, the watchtower, built.** Half the ground vertices that the shelters could stand on and whose land is raised half a cell above the plane hold a watchtower: a box tower of four stories inside its own edge, with sills, ledge, pinnacles, buttresses, a watch fire on the roof and a brazier by the door (`watchtower` in the planet; `planet:fixtures` names the fire). A box tower now takes how deep its foundation goes, since a foundation below the base is outside the edge that paints it. Law `structure/watchtower`, view `watchtower`. A first rule, a vertex higher than its four neighbours, found none: probably the neighbour frames were wrong; not traced.
- **D, the backrooms' stairwell and the well lining, built.** On a rooms level each of the four quarters around a backrooms well walls its side of the hole one cell out, and the quarter with the least face hash around the vertex (the rule the connector's foot uses) opens a door in its wall with a lit lintel (`stairwell` in `rooms.d`, `rooms:materials.sign`). Maze levels already wall the hole and are left alone. Under every ground with wells the shaft is lined in that ground's material from a depth that ground names, below what its own tiles paint (`caves-well`: `lining`, `lining-below`; city concrete below 12 cells, backrooms wet concrete below 31, vaults sandstone below 10), painted with `replace` so it never fills a passage. Views `backroomsstairwell` and `vaultswellbelow`, rendered and looked at. No law for the sign: its material is sunlight, which the biomes also use, so a search near the well would pass without it.
- **Hidden lights fixed.** `base:ember-light`, `tide-light` and `mist-light` belong to hidden families and draw nothing for an undosed player. This round had used them as the watch fire, the lights of the boiler and cistern biomes and the lit ceilings of the sealed glow and gypsum caves, so all of those were dark. They are now lava (the fire), sunlight and light panels. A new glowing material would need two recipes to pass `content-check`, which is why existing ones were taken.
- **I, fields and the subtype table, built.** Four fields per face, each a maximum of salted face hashes over its neighbourhood (grandeur and darkness one ring, wetness and age two), read from one gather of the 36 faces `strongestNear` already probed, so the tile probes no more than before. Every `rooms-biome` has an above and a below per field and a weight (the scout's table; the works level by wetness), and a face takes the nearest box, ties broken by a weighted roll on a key that is constant over the overlap of the field regions. The well rule asks the same function. Plan pools, rare rooms, uses and edges are not built. No law yet: scripts cannot read a face's biome. Rendered: offices next to a red-room region at the backrooms' start.
- **I, rare rooms, built.** A level lists rare set pieces (`rooms-rare`: a plan and a one-in), each rolled on its own per face and level from the face hash, below the forced plans (maze, lobby, the rooms around a stair) and above solid; a chapel still needs no hall crossing and a court the top level. The offices have a garden hall (all quarters courts, 1 in 27) and a drowned hall (pools, 1 in 27), the houses a pillared hall (1 in 27) and a ringed hall (1 in 81). `backroomscourt` rendered: several courts open to the sky side by side.
- **D, a tag for the standalone worlds' roots, built.** The `city` and `backrooms` worlds register their root as a solid kind with their own tag (`horospheres:city`, `horospheres:backrooms`, never rising) and root it, as the vaults world already did, so everything keyed by tag now reaches their own roots: the well's stair and lining, the port rule, the tile reach. `structure/well` now states that the city's well stair is steel. No engine change.
- **L, the hamlet, built in the planet.** A ground vertex is a hamlet's centre at `hamlet-odds` (2 in 100); a vertex on low ground (not raised half a cell) that is a centre or one ground edge from one has a shelter, so huts stand in clusters of up to five; each vertex decides from hashes alone (`probeHamlet`). View `wildhamlet`, rendered: three huts in sight of each other. No law: a script can find a bench, but not tell one hut from another without a second search that the helpers do not offer.
- **G3, endless girders, built.** `SitePart.line` (plugin ABI 35) hashes the whole straight line of edges an edge lies on: the edge's own seed without the term for how far along the line it is (`ensureLineSeed` in `structure.d`), so every edge of one line answers the same. The factor that mixes a part into a seed stays 3 (`SEEDED_PARTS`), so no vertex, face or edge hash moved. In the default world's void, every edge of one line in 81 (`colossus-one-in` in the lattice's settings) fills its whole column with gabbro, edge by edge, into air only (`colossus` in `lattice.d`), so a girder 24 cells square runs on through the void as far as the line does; one such line in three is hollow, a corridor six cells wide through it. The line hash is asked first and the pens only for a chosen line. Rendered with the odds at 1 in 3 for the look and put back: slabs that wall in whole cells. No law: none is guaranteed near spawn.
- **Checked at the end of these pieces:** 136 tests, 162 views, 9 films, `content-check` 0 problems, `check-repaint` `differing=0` on all ten worlds (planet 72201 tiles, city 3037, backrooms 3056, library 2554, vaults 3038, caves 29646, lattice 715410, the skies 1371 each), `check-forget` on lattice, all three programs built. The planet's lines now: `digest populated=10140 materials eed225f6dd7440e82ea5634c8f8dbbf2 occupancy 8e2f93abf447a9da63e5f54e333e21fc` and `deep populated=856 materials ed973b0f4bb1b5f7ea67a06d4a7fc902 occupancy cb66612cd92cfe7d8acd64877b5cfc42`. The `live/` tests 3.1, 2.7, 28.0 and 23.5 s; the bench with the profiler on, three runs, loads in 3.66, 3.55 and 3.56 s with 8, 6 and 8 late frames of 1800 (one run before these pieces: 3.60 s, 5). No old binary was kept to run beside, so whether the late frames rose is not settled.
- **L, the highways, built in the planet.** One straight ground line in 27 (`road-one-in`, by the line hash) is a road: every level ground edge on it paints its own length of road at the ground plane, eight cells of basalt over a gravel bed filled into air, a dashed white line down the middle, and the land cut away nine cells above it (`roadAlong` in the planet). A road stops eight cells short of a vertex that holds a shelter or watchtower, a cave mouth, a port, a rising ground or a landmark's clearing, so it keeps out of their cells. Rendered with the odds at 1 in 3 for the look and put back (`highway`): a road cut into a rise, two of them meeting at a vertex. The `live/` flights unchanged, three runs each (27.5, 27.5, 29.1 s with the library tests slowed alike; 23.5, 23.6, 24.3). No law: none is guaranteed near spawn. Not built: piers where a road crosses a valley, and huts as posts along it.
- **H4, the air, built.** Sealed means kept in the air too: where the ground nearest a node is a built one (flat fill) and the node lies more than 1.9 edges under it (where sealed caves begin), a collector gathers at three times the rate the air would give (`sealedGainAt` in pharmacology's `player.d`; `SEALED_GAIN`). It reads the planes a node already carries and adds no engine call. Law `structure/sealedair` (the street of the city: gain 1; 2.6 units down: 3), and `plant/collector` holds as before.
- **I, plan pools, built.** A plan is a content node (`rooms-plan`: rooms, pillars, pool or court, and the pillars' spacing), and a biome lists weighted plans in place of its pillars, pool and court odds; the weight is a field of the edge, which the binding now reads (`Weighted!T` in `plugins/api/binding.d`, no ABI change). The rows were written to give every biome its old mix (a court row below the top level stands as rooms, as before); the gallery's pillars stand six apart now (`rooms:wide-pillars`). 137 tests pass, the backrooms repaint clean.
- **I, edges, built.** A tile works out, once, the biome of each of its five neighbouring faces on every level (`besideOf` in `layout.d`: the same fields from each neighbour's own ball), and a quarter whose outer wall line borders a face of another biome frames its own door slots in its trim, a post each side and a lintel (`threshold` in `rooms.d`); each side frames its own half, so the two agree. On the offices the outer walls along halls have no doors, so the borders show on the other levels. The backrooms tests took the same wall time with it as without, three runs each (1.9, 1.9, 1.9 s against 1.9, 2.0, 1.8; `triprooms` 3.7, 3.7, 3.7 against 3.7, 3.6). Seen in a render with the frames marked in a glowing block for the look and put back: frames on doorways across the level.
- **B, breaches, built.** A vertex under open ground up to a quarter edge past the underside is kept, at one in two (`breach-odds`), when a walkable vertex above it is kept the ordinary way; its floor is then the void, since floors are laid only into rock. Where the crust under spawn is two edges thin, chambers end at 1.5 edges and none reached the underside before; now the lowest of them break through. The planet's `deep` digest line moved (`aae885d10d284f0cdcee4edab327cfab`, `eac90007ad4988154f2896648d5cdc34`), which fits a breach in the column under spawn (inferred); `under25` rendered shows a chamber in the rock open below to the void. Planet and caves repaint clean, the `live/` flights unchanged (27.4, 23.6 s). No law: a script cannot find a chamber's floor.
- **I, uses, built.** A biome lists what is stocked in it (`rooms-use`: a material, the odds per room, a height), and each room rolls each row and stacks it against a wall away from the doors, as the boxes always were (`stack` in `rooms.d`, which the boxes now go through too). The archive stocks steel and pallets, the office lamps, the attic pallets. No law (steel is also the vents and the roof), not rendered.
- **G4, the fractal layer, built.** From `fractal-layer` (5 edges, the boundary roughened by the same hash as the lattice's) below the underside, the planet hands an edge in the open to `branch` in `lattice.d`. Every vertex's parent is its neighbour with the highest vertex hash, if that is higher than its own, so the vertices make a forest whose roots are local maxima; an edge is a limb when one end is the other's parent, and its thickness is five cells less one for every step its child end lies from the trunk (walked at most four steps). Limbs are marble, filled into air only. View `under90`, rendered: white limbs of every thickness in the dark, a trunk across half the frame. Both `live/` flights the same as before, three runs each (27.6, 27.7 and 29.5 s, the last with the library tests slowed alike; 23.6, 23.9, 24.2), and the planet's repaint clean. The `deep` digest line moved again: `b78f3b60f8f8c6cb4a011de75e492b08` and `849ae255ed3b7a7b51211f6b7c7ce792`.
- **What this round's planet features cost, measured on one binary.** With the girders, highways, hamlets, fractal layer and breaches switched off through their settings and on again, alternating builds, the bench with the profiler on read: off 3.40 and 3.45 s to load with 6 and 12 late frames of 1800; on 3.55, 3.54, 3.57 and 3.48 s with 13 and 7. Late frames are the same; the load is about a tenth of a second slower, which follows the extra buildings near spawn (3599 against 3333; the roads about 150 of them, the hamlets most of the rest), not the breaches (3.56 s without them). By section 4 that is a failure; it is the price of more to see around spawn, and the odds that set it are in the planet's settings.
- **A fault, misread at first.** In one configuration two bench runs out of two stopped in flight with "paints ground that is already built: a tile paints only near its face (tile over horosphere 10040, segment 0)". That message is `PaintResult.behind` (a paint into a node already built), not `beyond` as was written here; a reach given to the risen wild ground was taken as the cause and changed, and two runs passed after it, which was luck. The same refusal came back on the face tiles of risen grounds, about one bench run in three; the cause and the fix are under "The 'already built' fault, traced and fixed" below. The wild ground has no reach again, as before the round. The refusal now says which tile (a ground's or the edge's) and the ground's tag, and a paint outside the reach is reported as such before the built test is asked (`TilePainter.paint`).
- **The three debates, settled by fresh agents as the owner's rules ask.** J4: a catalog desk first, the walk later; every compound has a call number of 28 base-nine digits (the conjugation bit, then each cell's fragment), a shape's 24 turnings share one shelf (the least packed value among them), and what it does to the reader shows only with the assay kit. G6: walkways in a face-plane of the honeycomb picked by a plane hash (a new `SitePart.plane`), no new kind of ground; but the agent worked out that any such plane is flat only near its foot and tilts past 50 degrees an edge from it, a curtain into the abyss, and that a flat sheet at a fixed depth is not on the honeycomb at all, so the owner has to say which is meant before it is built (question 6 of section 8, made concrete). L0: the crown, the palace and the trail recur through the planet's own edge tile on footings, decided by hashes, with no engine change: a crown's ring of towers with its decks at its first story (the edges an edge up are above the planet's edge ceiling), a palace of one face in a hollow, a trail along a hash forest on the ground from every cave mouth to a hut or a cairn.
- **J4, the catalog desk, built.** `pharmacology:catalog` fills a new role; every atrium's first quarter in the library holds one at the corner of its desk. Using it opens a screen of the shape's three layers of nine cells (each cycles through empty and the eight property sets), a conjugation switch, the shelf's call number, the half-life, and with the assay kit the receptors it would act on (`catalog.d`, `buildCatalog` in `panels.d`; the reading is taken in the step while the desk is open). A workbench makes one from nine planks and three glass. Laws `gene/catalog` (all 24 turnings of a shape read one call number) and `structure/librarycatalog` (the nearest desk opens where it stands). View `catalog`, rendered. Not built: the doorway marks that spell a shelf by walking, and keeping the desk's shape in the save.
- **Fixed on the way:** `structure/inlay` read its stone from the well test's constant and broke when that became steel; it has its own now. `horospheres.d` had two statements on one line.
- **Not built, because each needs an answer first:** J3's rumor engine (the texts need the owner); G6, the hanging maze (curtain or flat sheet, above).
- **L0, the trail, built in the planet.** Every ground vertex points at the neighbour along the ground with the highest trail rank, if that is higher than its own, so the vertices make a forest; a level ground edge whose ends are child and parent, with a cave mouth at most three steps behind the child, is a trail: a gravel path two cells wide that follows the land's relief, the land cleared four cells over it, stopping short of a homestead it does not lead to, and a cairn of andesite where the forest's root ends it (`trailAlong`, `layTrail`, `paintTrail`). Planet repaint clean.
- **L0, the crown, built in the planet.** A face is a crown at `crown-odds` (3 in 100) when each of its five corners lies within five cells of the plane and holds no mouth, port, landmark clearing or rising: a tower in every corner's quarter, sixteen cells high, with a stair up its inside to a deck at twelve cells, walks along the face's edges at the deck, crenellations and a lamp (`crownQuarter`). A first rule also refused corners with a shelter or watchtower; with towers at half the raised vertices no face passed it, so crowns stand beside homesteads. Rendered at odds 1: rings of towers all around spawn.
- **L0, the palace, built in the planet.** The palace's halls, stairs, basin and a new dome are shared code (`plugins/palace/palace.d`, with the debug world and landmark in a `palace-mod` library). A face is a palace at `palace-odds` (2 in 100) when its corners lie within four cells of the plane and are free as for a crown and of homesteads too, since the halls fill the quarters at the vertices; then a crown is not tried. The recurring palace stands on the ground (`standingQuarter`): a hall per quarter with outer walls, windows and merlons, the basin and a stepped stone dome over the face's centre, and one door with an apron at the corner whose vertex hash is least; a footing below (`standingFooting`). The debug palace also has the dome and merlons. Rendered at odds 1.
- **Where they are painted.** Both were first painted by the wild ground's face tile, and the bench then stopped two runs in three with "paints ground that is already built (a ground's tile over horosphere 0)": a face tile's paints at a vertex are filed on that vertex's line, and a node beside the line that never asks for the face's tile can be built first (inferred from where it failed, not traced). They are painted now as the shelters are, by the planet's edge tile along the vertical edge at a ground vertex, for each of the four faces around it (`standingAt`, `uprightAt`): the edge standing up paints everything at or above the plane, the edge hanging down only the footings. The two tiles may see a face's quarter mirrored, which is why everything below the plane is symmetric. The root fault has not come back in the runs since.
- **The "already built" fault, traced and fixed.** A diagnostic at the refusal showed the built node on the foot line and within reach, but with the risen ground missing from the node's own list of grounds, so it had never asked for that ground's tile. The list was wrong because of `TileHost.run`: when a tile task on a worker needs a ground that does not exist yet, its view is blocked and everything it discovers after that is incomplete; the streamer and the tile planner drop a blocked view's discoveries (`view.forget()`), the tile run did not, and `ThreadViews.finish` merged the incomplete lists into real nodes. It now drops them. The same refusal also stopped all tile painting for the rest of the session (buildings fell to 0, the whole underground was drawn, late frames went from about 5 to 33), so it was a world-breaking fault in play, not only a failed bench. Before the fix the bench failed about one run in three; after it, eight runs out of eight passed, loading in 3.59 to 3.67 s with 8 late frames of 1800 in the run read and no mesh short of faces. Two fuller repairs were tried and taken out for their cost: running a blocked tile again on the main thread (load 4.0 to 4.2 s, 20 late frames, and 12 meshes short of faces after the flight) and again on the workers in further rounds (4.55 s, 34 late frames). So a blocked tile's own paint is still used as it was before this round; only its discoveries are dropped. Earlier guesses recorded in this section (the wild ground's reach, the foot line) were wrong and are undone.
- **Roads and trails keep out of palaces; the palace door sits on the land.** A road or trail is not laid along an edge with a palace face on either side (`isBesidePalace`, `groundAlong`): the road is eight cells wide and would cut the palace's wall. Crowns need nothing: their towers start five cells from the edge and their walks are twelve up, clear of a road's eight by nine. A palace's door is at the corner where the land is highest, a face is a palace only if that corner is no lower than half a cell under the plane (`PALACE_DOOR_LOWEST`), and steps are cut outward from the door up to the land, six cells.
- **The recurring palace is sunk, as the debug one is.** Its floor lies eleven cells under the plane and its roof eleven above, split at the plane between the two edge tiles at each vertex: the edge standing up paints walls, windows, merlons, ceiling, roof, dome and the door (`upperQuarter`), the edge hanging down the floor, the lower walls, the carpet, basin and jet, and the stair (`lowerQuarter`). Since the two tiles may see the quarter mirrored, the door corner has a door in each of its two walls and a stair down along each, a two cell landing and nine one cell treads, which is what a quarter of twelve cells holds (a first stair of the debug palace's length left its column and stopped the world). Rendered at odds 1: from under the ceiling, the hall floor far below with carpet and water and the windows at ground level; and with a view that searched from the ground for the stair's white brick, the stair at one corner and no other.
- **Trails stop short of a mouth.** A trail ran right to a cave mouth and its gravel bed, filled into air, bridged the sinkhole (`structure/sinkhole` fell 0.11 where it needs 0.9); a trail now stops twelve cells short of a mouth at either end (`TRAIL_MOUTH`).
- **Checked at the end of the round,** after the fault's fix, the road and door changes, the sunk palace, the brush-drawn connector, the review's cleanups and the three review items built: 139 tests, 165 views, 9 films, `content-check` 0 problems, `check-repaint` `differing=0` on all ten worlds (planet 73149 tiles, the rest as before), `check-forget` on lattice, all three programs built.
